<summary>The title page of a late 18th-century edition of Joannes Luiken’s iconic emblem book, which explores the mystical relationship between the soul and the divine.</summary>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page serves as the "Brief Message to the Reader," a standard preface that introduces the spiritual themes of the book. It establishes the central metaphor of the text as a garden of "emblems" gathered from various holy sources.</meta>

## BRIEF MESSAGE
### TO THE
# R E A D E R.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut drop cap 'N' featuring acanthus leaf and floral patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>
No one who enters the garden of these emblems <note>Zinnebeelden: symbolic images or poems used to illustrate a moral or religious lesson</note> should think that all these little flowers have grown from our own soil: the majority, which are of the highest color and the sweetest scent, we have gathered <note>original: "geleezen" — this Dutch term punningly means both "read" and "harvested/gleaned"</note> from the gardens of the high-worthy friends of God; those who have attained the contemplative and united life, and have been planted here for the delight of the spirit.

There are many, sprouted from a most noble soil, which contain a very sweet honey for the hungry mind <note>Gemoed: the inner heart or seat of the soul's emotions</note>, which, like a little honeybee, does not only take pleasure in the elegance of the flowers, but draws its necessary nourishment from them.

There are some here to awaken the soul from the sleep of sin; others to refresh the soul in its dejection, anxiety, and temptation; others to make it fall in love with the Origin <note>Oorſprong: referring to God as the source of all things</note> of all beauties. Some smell of precious truth; some of love; some lure the soul to union with God: there are also those which, with their noble scent and color, show us a glimpse of the joyful Paradise; an eternal pleasure-garden <note>luſtgaarde: a garden of delights</note> of the angels and blessed souls.

<vocab>Emblems, Little Flowers, Little Honeybee, Soul, Origin, Paradise, Pleasure-garden</vocab>

<summary>In this preface, the author introduces the book as a metaphorical garden of spiritual "emblems" gathered from the writings of mystics and "friends of God." The text encourages the reader to act like a honeybee, extracting spiritual nourishment from these symbolic "flowers" to find comfort and union with the divine.</summary>
<keywords>preface, emblems, spirituality, mysticism, garden metaphor, soul's journey, divine love</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Brief Message to the Reader" by Jan Luyken, clarifying the symbolic nature of the illustrations (emblems) and expressing the author's personal humility and spiritual hope.</meta>

In this Rose Garden <note>Roozenhof: literally a "court of roses," used here as a title for the collection of spiritual emblems</note> you will generally see JESUS and the SOUL depicted in prints; but the observer must never imagine while reading (whenever the Eternal Divinity is spoken of) that we mean anything with these pictorial representations other than the physical, figurative Humanity of *Christ*, in His assumed form of a servant <note>Knechtelyke gestalte: a biblical reference to Philippians 2:7, where Christ takes on human form</note>, just as He walked here on Earth among us humans, visible and tangible to external eyes and hands. For according to His Eternal Divinity, He neither may nor can be depicted.

And, as I have said above, if you find something high <note>something "high" refers to sublime or advanced spiritual states</note> here—as there is indeed something high in some places—do not think that I have experienced it myself. Oh no! But I accept it with joy as precious truth, and desire that it may be essentially fulfilled in me, and in all people: And that we ourselves, according to our <term>inner ground</term> <note>inwendigen grond: a mystical term referring to the deepest, most essential part of the soul where it meets God</note>, may become a graceful Garden, planted with many kinds of fragrant Herbs and Flowers; purified of all stinging Thistles and Thorns, so that He who grazes among the Lilies (JESUS, blessed into eternity) may delight to dwell with us and in us.

->J. LUIKEN.<-

<summary>In this concluding part of the preface, Jan Luyken explains that the physical depictions of Jesus in his emblems represent Christ's humanity rather than His invisible divinity. He humbly clarifies that he writes of high spiritual states not from personal mastery, but as a shared goal for all souls to become a "spiritual garden" fit for God.</summary>

<keywords>Jan Luyken, Rose Garden, Jesus, Soul, Humanity of Christ, Eternal Divinity, Inner Ground, Spiritual Garden, Emblems, Mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page follows the "Brief Message to the Reader," where Jan Luyken introduced the spiritual nature of his emblems. Having established that the images represent the human side of Christ and the inner state of the soul, he now begins the poetic portion of the book with an opening call to spiritual awakening.</meta>

# PRELUDE.

Return! return! O straying Soul!
So far from Home, lost in a wilderness;
Where cunning deceit follows on your heels,
And weaves a net of luxury and fair appearance:
Just as in the forest a **Fowler** <note>Vogelaar: a bird-catcher. In 17th-century spiritual art, the fowler was a common symbol for the Devil or worldly temptation laying traps for the innocent soul.</note> goes wandering,
Lurking for the little Dove, deceptively whistling and flattering,
To pull the cunning Net over her head;
O poor Soul! so are you also led astray.
From hour to hour you come closer to misery;
What has so bewitched your understanding,
That you do not think of God, your dear Father,
And **Paradise**, your true Fatherland?
Do you belong here at home in this miserable life?
Consider well, whether you do not find yourself
To be an exile, driven from estate and land:
Open your eyes, and be no longer blind.
How mad you are, to run into destruction!
Your **Bridegroom**, the fairest who lives,
Calls you to him, with both his arms open:
It does not please him <note>haagd hem niet: it does not please or suit Him; He takes no delight in your straying.</note> that you thus wander and drift:
He loved you, and chose you for his Bride:
He bought your freedom; but not with worldly goods:
A higher price was destined for your salvation:
He gave for you, so generously, his own blood.
Is that a small thing? Can this not draw your will?
Can this love, in your chilled Heart,
Waken no flame, nor spark of mutual love?
What pain did he not suffer for your sake!
One who might have such a Lover, and Bridegroom, yes, King,
Would they so wretchedly and wickedly
Go wandering in eternity without a crowning,
As a **Slave-woman**? <note>Slaavin: referring to the soul being enslaved to sin or worldly desires, rather than reigning as a "bride" in heaven.</note> That pity were too great.
Yet it is time to return once more.
Be not dismayed, if someone speaks to you:

<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: Meend</meta>

<summary>This opening poem, or Prelude, serves as an urgent call for the "straying soul" to turn away from the distractions of the world and return to God. Using the metaphor of a fowler trapping a dove, Luyken warns against the deceits of life and reminds the reader of Christ's sacrifice as a loving Bridegroom.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, conversion, Fowler metaphor, Bridegroom, Christ's sacrifice, spiritual exile, Paradise</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Prelude," a poetic appeal to the soul to abandon worldly distractions and return to the divine Bridegroom. The text transitions from the previous page's warning about the "Fowler" (the Devil) to an assurance of God's mercy and a description of the beauty of the celestial realm. The catchword "JEZUS" suggests the next page begins with a focus on Christ himself.</meta>

Do you think that God will still desire you,
While you are mired so deep in unfaithfulness?
Be not discouraged. If you hate your old life:
If you desire Him, He cannot elude you;
He will forget and forgive your guilt,
And take you once more to be His **Bride**. <note>The "Bride" is a central image in 17th-century Dutch mysticism, representing the human soul (Anima) joined in a spiritual marriage to Christ.</note>
He loves you, O Soul, and shall marry you.
You shall receive a crown, and become a Queen;
A wedding feast shall be held there long, yes, eternally.
Why do you still dally <note>sloft: literally "to shuffle" or "loiter"; the poet asks why the reader moves so sluggishly toward such a magnificent reward.</note> over such a great gain!
In **Paradise** it is fairer than on Earth;
There the beautiful **Seraphim** <note>Serafyn: the highest order of angels, traditionally associated with light, purity, and a burning love for the Divine.</note> walks and stands,
And weaves a wreath from this **Rose-garden**, <note>Roozegaarde: often used as a metaphor for the heavenly state or the garden of virtues in the heart.</note>
That shall be a crown upon his head.
That rose-red looks lovely against golden hair,
Around the face, so friendly and so sweet,
As if the daylight seemed to brighten from within it;
Then he praises God, with power from his heart.
So beautiful, O Soul, so beautiful are your companions,
When you arrive in the true Fatherland.
How could earthly beauty not weary us?
How could we not set it aside?
Return! Return! O straying Soul!
So far from home, lost in a wilderness,
Where cunning deceit follows at your heels,
Of which the end would be **Death** and **Hell**.

<summary>This concluding section of the Prelude offers the soul hope of divine forgiveness and describes the radiant beauty of the afterlife to encourage the reader to abandon worldly sin.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, Bride of Christ, Seraphim, Paradise, Repentance, Heavenly Marriage, Spiritual Awakening</keywords>

# JESUS AND THE SOUL.

### The Soul Awakened by the Drawing <note>trek: a theological term describing the "inner pull" or divine influence God exerts on the human heart.</note> of the FATHER.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial A featuring floral and foliage motifs.</image-desc>
ALTHOUGH in this land, there is such a mild abundance
For the external fire of life,
A surplus to burn without a care,
And flesh and blood—that coarse beast—
To give full rein to its earthly lusts;
Yet I often feel, within my heart,
A sparking fire, a deeply hidden life,
That hungers for a higher good;
And says: We are not at home on this earth;
I thirst for my Fatherland,
And the Father who birthed me from His essence,
Against which the Donkey <note>The "donkey" or "ass" was a common 17th-century emblem representing the stubborn, animalistic nature of the human body which resists spiritual impulses.</note> opposes itself:
"There is still time enough; away with all too deep concerns:
Fulfill yet this one last desire."
This drives the Flesh, from one morning to the next,
And lulls the Spirit into a false rest.
Now no longer. Now is the time for waking:
Before the Soul becomes a thistle,
And may never reach grace again,
O Lord! give me another heart.

## Divine Answer.

*Let the wicked forsake his way, and the unrighteous*
*man his thoughts, and let him return to the*
*Lord, and He will have mercy on him. Isaiah 55: 7.*
*Today, if you hear His voice, do not harden*
*your hearts. Hebrews 3: 15.*

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "drawing" of the Father introduced on the previous page. While page 11 established the external contrast between earthly abundance and spiritual hunger, page 12 moves inward, identifying the "hidden will" as the direct action of God calling the soul back to its original state.</meta>

# JESUS AND THE SOUL.

## On the 1st Emblem.

->*Of the drawing of the Father, and how man*<-
->*must follow it.*<-

When a person finds a deeply hidden will within themselves, which would gladly turn to God’s grace if only they could, let them know that this very Will is that embodied Word of God, spoken in Paradise after the sins were committed: that the God JEHOVAH, as the Father, still draws him to Christ. For in our own self-nature <note>original: "eigenheid," a term often used in mystical literature to describe the fallen, ego-driven state of humanity apart from God</note>, we no longer have any will toward obedience.

But this same drawing of the Father, as that embodied, spoken grace, draws all people, even the most godless, provided they are not entirely a thistle <note>a biblical metaphor for a heart that is prickly, stubborn, and bears no fruit</note>, and are willing to pause for a moment from their false activities to allow the drawing to work.

Thus, no person has cause to doubt God’s grace, if they find within themselves a desire to be converted <note>original: "bekeeren," meaning to turn around or return to God</note> even once.

Let that person not delay for a single moment more, as it is written: Today, when you hear the voice of the Lord, do not harden your hearts. <note>A reference to Psalm 95:7-8 and Hebrews 3:15</note>

For that desire to eventually be converted is God’s voice within the human being—which the Devil, with his introduced images <note>original: "ingevoerde Beelden," referring to worldly distractions, fantasies, or idols that the mind prioritizes over spiritual truth</note>, covers up and delays, so that it is pushed forward from one day and one year to the next—

<meta>catchword: word,</meta>

<summary>This page explains the theological concept of the "drawing" of the Father, asserting that any internal desire to turn toward God is evidence of divine grace rather than human merit. It warns the reader not to let worldly distractions or "images" provided by the Devil delay their spiritual conversion.</summary>
<keywords>drawing of the Father, grace, free will, self-nature, conversion, Jehovah, Christ, spiritual awakening, repentance</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 12, describing how the devil's distractions delay the soul's conversion until it becomes hardened. The Dutch text transitions from "zo dat" (so that) on page 12 to "word" (becomes) at the start of page 13.</meta>

### THE FIRST PART.

...becomes, until finally the Soul becomes a thistle <note>Continuing the metaphor from the previous page: a heart so hardened and prickly that it can no longer receive the "dew" of grace</note>, and can no longer reach that grace.

Take heed, do not block your heart <note>original: "gemoed," referring to one's inner disposition or the seat of emotions and thoughts</note> and understanding. When your heart speaks: "Turn back, do not do this evil," then know that you are being called by the wisdom of God; turn back quickly, and think of where your home is, in what a heavy house of bondage <note>original: "dienst-huize," an allusion to the "house of slavery" in Egypt, representing the soul's captivity to sin</note> your Soul lies imprisoned, and search for your Fatherland <note>original: "Vaderland," a common mystical term for the soul's true origin in the divine</note> from which your Soul has wandered away, and where it ought to return.

O Soul! O eternal creature, take heed of the time of grace while the door stands open to you. See, a voice calls you from your sleep; raise yourself up, rub the sleep <note>original: "vaak," an archaic Dutch word for slumber or sleepiness</note> from your eyes, and see that the Sun shall soon go down, while you are here in the open field, still so far from home. Spring to your feet, and step forward quickly without looking back, before the eternal night falls upon you; for the Sun of divine grace would not rise for you again, and you would have to walk forever in the dismal darkness. That would be too great a pity! To be eternally deprived of light, of joy, of Father, and of Fatherland.

What, after all, are the luxury and pleasure <note>original: "weelde en wellust," suggesting the intoxicating but empty indulgences of worldly life</note> of this world? Like a shadow they pass by; like smoke and vapor they disappear, leaving their lovers with nothing but a gnawing worm <note>A reference to the "worm that dieth not" (Mark 9:48), symbolizing the enduring regret of the conscience</note> in the heart; everything must be left behind here, yet they take that "treasure" with them into eternity. For a moment of trivial joy, an eternal Kingdom is neglected. God preserve us from such folly.

<summary>This page warns the soul against spiritual procrastination, urging the reader to recognize God's internal call and return to their spiritual "Fatherland" before the metaphorical sun of grace sets. It contrasts the fleeting, vapor-like pleasures of the world with the eternal weight of a neglected salvation.</summary>

<keywords>soul, grace, heart, wisdom of God, Fatherland, spiritual awakening, house of bondage, eternal night, worldly pleasure, Kingdom of God</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the themes of spiritual warfare and the soul's liberation from the worldly "house of bondage" mentioned on the previous page. It transitions from a prose warning to a poetic dialogue between the Soul and the Flesh.</meta>

# JESUS AND THE SOUL.

### The Soul Sets Itself Against Flesh and Blood.

"Would you then so completely forsake me,
My bosom friend? <note>original: "halsvriendin," literally "neck-friend," meaning an intimate companion who embraces one's neck</note> O Soul, and rob me
Of my highest good, my luxuriant, playful life,
And lead me into a deep slavery?
Would you violate your friend, your closest companion?
And make me a scorn and mockery to everyone,
And cast me into much sorrow and misery;
Consider carefully, can that be right before God?"
So speaks the Flesh, the **old man** <note>original: "den ouden mensch," a reference to the Apostle Paul's description of the unregenerate human nature in Romans 6:6</note> of sins.
Hear the answer I give him:
"It is better that you be bound for a time,
Than that you should drive me into eternal bonds:
You are a rogue, and weave nothing but misery for me:
You care only for your belly, and act like a beast;
And when you come to your besieged end,
Because your foundation lies in the **Spirit of the Stars**, <note>A term from the mystical philosophy of Jacob Böhme; it refers to the "astral" or "sidereal" body, which is physical and temporary, influenced by the planets and destined to perish, unlike the eternal soul</note>
You then break apart, and let me drift away;
If God's light does not shine in my soul’s fire,
Then I must surely wander in eternal darkness,
Because I spring from eternal nature;
Therefore, O guest, the watch is commanded to me;
You scatter into nothingness, I am an eternal Bond,
I have no desire to wander for eternity,
I seek my rest in the eternal **Fatherland**."

### Divine Answer.

If your right hand offends you, cut it off, and cast it from you. *Matth. V: 30.*
Mortify <note>original: "Doodet," literally "kill"</note> therefore your members which are upon the Earth. *Kol. III: 5.*

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>A poetic dialogue where the Soul rejects the pleas of the "Flesh" (the sinful physical self), arguing that the body is temporary and governed by the stars, while the soul must seek its eternal home in God to avoid darkness.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, Flesh, Spiritual Warfare, Spirit of the Stars, Eternal Fatherland, Old Man of Sin, Jacob Böhme, Jan Luyken, asceticism, immortality</keywords>

<meta>catchword: Upon</meta>

It utilizes the mystical concept of the "Spirit of the Stars" to explain the temporary nature of the body versus the eternal nature of the spirit.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the spiritual dialogue between the Soul and the physical self, specifically focusing on the internal conflict described in the previous page. # JESUS AND THE SOUL.

### *The Soul Sets Itself Against Flesh and Blood.*

"Would you then so completely and entirely forsake me,
My bosom friend? <note>original: "halsvriendin," literally "neck-friend," meaning an intimate companion who embraces one's neck</note> O Soul, and rob me
Of my highest good, my luxuriant, playful life,
And lead me into a deep slavery?
Would you violate your friend, your closest companion?
And make me a scorn and mockery to everyone,
And cast me into much sorrow and misery;
Consider carefully, can that be right before God?"
So speaks the Flesh, the **old man** <note>original: "den ouden mensch," a reference to the Apostle Paul's description of the unregenerate human nature in Romans 6:6</note> of sins.
Hear the answer I give him:
"It is better that you be bound for a time,
Than that you should drive me into eternal bonds:
You are a rogue, and weave <note>original: "spint," literally "spins" like a thread</note> nothing but misery for me:
You care only for your belly, and act like a beast;
And when you come to your besieged end,
Because your foundation lies in the **Spirit of the Stars**, <note>original: "Sterren Geest." In 17th-century mystical thought, this refers to the sidereal or astral spirit—the part of man's nature tied to the physical cosmos which perishes at death</note>
Then you break apart, and let me depart;
If God's light does not shine in my **Soul-fire**;
Then I must wander in eternal darkness,
Because I originate from eternal nature;
Therefore, O guest, I have been commanded to keep watch;
You turn to dust, <note>original: "stuift tot niet," literally "blows away to nothing"</note> but I am an eternal Bond,
I have no desire to wander for eternity,
I seek my rest in the **eternal Fatherland**."

---

## Divine Answer.

> *If your right hand causes you to sin, cut it off, and*
> *cast it from you. Matthew 5:30.*
> *Mortify <note>Put to death</note> therefore your members which are upon the earth. Colossians 3:5.*

# JESUS AND THE SOUL.

## On the Second Emblem. <note>original: "Zinnebeeld," literally a sense-image, used to describe an emblem or symbolic illustration.</note>

### *Of the struggle between Spirit and Flesh.*

The **old Adam** <note>A theological term for the unregenerate human nature, often contrasted with Christ, the "New Adam."</note> speaks: O poor Soul! why do you not seek the world's favor and friendship? Why do you not strive after temporary honor, after pleasure and wealth, so that it may go well with you in this world? Why do you make yourself a fool of the world, and why are you everyone’s Owl <note>original: "Uil." In 17th-century Dutch culture, the owl was often a symbol of a fool or someone who is mocked because they cannot see in the "light" of common sense or worldliness.</note> and doormat? Why do you let yourself be despised by those who are lesser and know less than you? Could you not also trot along with the hypocrites? Then you would be beloved, and no harm would come to you; you would be more secure in your body and goods than on this path, where you are but the world’s Owl and Fool.

But the WISDOM of God speaks: You, my dear Soul, whom I have chosen, go with me; I am not of this world. I want to lead you out of this world into my kingdom, where there is pure, soft rest and well-being; in my kingdom there is pure joy, honor, and glory: there is no Taskmaster <note>original: "Dryver," referring to a harsh overseer or a slave-driver; here it represents the devil or the pressures of worldly demands.</note> there. I want to adorn you with God’s glory and clothe you in my beautiful ornament: I want to make you a Lord in Heaven and a judge over this world; you shall help judge the Taskmaster in his wickedness: he shall lie as a footstool <note>original: "ſchabel," a low stool or footstool.</note> for your feet. He shall not open his mouth against you, but shall be eternally locked away in his grim wrath: you shall eat from my table: there shall be no envy or lack: my fruit is sweeter and lovelier than the fruit of this world, from which you never have any pain. All your doing shall be friendly laughing and lovely speaking: before you shall appear pure humility in great love. Your playmates <note>original: "geſpeelen," referring to spiritual companions or angels.</note> are indeed so beautiful, you shall have joy in them all:

<summary>This page presents a prose commentary on the second emblem, depicting a spiritual debate where the "Old Adam" tempts the Soul with worldly security while Divine Wisdom promises eternal rest and exaltation.</summary>
<keywords>Old Adam, Wisdom of God, spiritual struggle, temptation, worldliness, heavenly kingdom, Taskmaster, Soul's journey</keywords>

This reflects the 17th-century mystical view that the soul is an eternal spark trapped in a temporary, "beast-like" physical form that must be transformed or shed.</meta>

# THE FIRST PART.

B

<meta>catchword: am:</meta>

...am: <note>The text continues from the previous page: "I want to lead you... for I am:"</note> Why do you value that perishable life? You shall enter into an imperishable one that lasts forever.

<margin>* Flesh and blood, the old man.</margin>
If then great honor meets you in place of your scorn, why do you mourn? Rise up like a flower from the earth out of your **Animal** <note>original: "Dier." In this mystical context, the "Animal" refers to the biological, instinctual body and its mortal nature, often contrasted with the immortal Spirit.</note> *.

<margin>† Wellspring of Life.</margin>
That wild **Animal** speaks to the **Soul**: I am nonetheless your Animal, and in me you were born; had I not grown, you would not have been either. But the **Soul** answers: Hear, my Animal, I am greater than you; before you were to be, I was your master-builder; my highest powers are from the root of **eternity**, but you are of this world and you perish, yet I live in my † **Source** <note>original: "Wel," meaning a spring or fountainhead of existence.</note> eternally, therefore I am nobler than you. You live in a wrathful Source; but I want to place my wrathful Source into the light, into that eternal joy. My works stand in power, but yours remain only in the **figure** <note>original: "figuur." A term used to describe the outward, temporary shape or shadow of a thing, as opposed to its eternal essence.</note>. Once I am released from you, I will not take you back as my Animal, but rather my new body, which I bear within you, in your deepest root of the **holy Element** <note>In the mystical tradition of Jakob Böhme, the "holy Element" refers to a spiritual, heavenly corporality that is pure and undivided, unlike the four conflicting elements of the physical world.</note>. I no longer want your rough offspring of the four **Elements**; death swallows you: but I turn green with my new body out of you, like a flower from its root. I want to forget you: for God's glory, which cursed you along with the earth, has grafted my root back into his Son, and my body grows in the holy Element before God; therefore you are but my wild Animal, which here hurts and plagues me, upon which the **Devil** rides as on his cursed horse. And if the world mocks you, I do not heed it; it does so for my sake, yet it cannot see me and does not know me; why then is it so mad? It cannot murder me, for I am not in it.

<meta>This page continues the spiritual journey of the Soul. On the previous page, the Soul asserted its eternal nature over the "Animal" (the mortal body). Now, on page 18, the Soul turns its gaze outward to the natural world, using the beauty of the physical creation as a ladder to contemplate the even greater beauty of the Creator. This is a classic theme in mystical literature: finding the "traces" of the divine in the material world.</meta>

# JESUS AND THE SOUL.

->*The Soul contemplates the Creator through the Created Things.*<-

I saw the beauty and the sweetness of all things,
And said, "How beautiful you are!" Then my soul heard:
"So we are indeed; but He, from whom we received it all,
Is a thousand times more beautiful, and a thousand times more sweet."
And that is You, my Beloved; should I not desire You?
If here on earth a lily petal is so white and fine,
What, O eternal Good, O Lord of all things,
What must the whiteness of Your purity then be!
Is the purple <note>original: "Purper." In the 17th century, "purple" often referred to a deep, royal red, particularly the color of high-quality roses.</note> of the roses that bloom here also so beautiful,
Bedewed with pearls <note>A poetic metaphor for morning dew.</note> as the morning sun greets them?
How the purple of Your Majesty must then glow!
If a violet smells so lovely and so sweet here,
As the west wind gently wanders through the gardens,
As I have often found on a cool morning:
What a fragrance must then spread through Paradise,
Flowing so lovely from Your kind mouth!
I see the Sun here, risen like a bridegroom <note>A reference to Psalm 19:5, comparing the sun to a bridegroom coming out of his chamber.</note>,
So beautiful and shining at the height of the day;
How bright and clear must Your countenance then be!
O God! My most beautiful Beloved, if only I might see You once!

### Divine Answer.

Blessed [are] the pure in heart: For they shall see God. Matthew 5:8.

The page concludes with a scriptural promise that the pure in heart will eventually behold this divine beauty.</summary>

-> JESUS AND THE SOUL. <-
<meta>catchword: het</meta>

## On the III. Emblem.

### *Of inward and outward things.*

The outward is a manifestation of the inward. When I speak, those outward words are a revelation of the inward word that arises in my heart. This visible world is nothing other than an out-birthing <note>original: "uitgeboorte." This mystical term suggests that the physical world was "born out" of a spiritual source, rather than just being constructed.</note> of the inward world; everything we see here is also present inwardly in the invisible realm. Just as we find light and darkness here, so there is also an eternal light and an eternal darkness inwardly—though these are not changeable like day and night. Instead, that darkness is eternally hidden within the light, just as this outward darkness, beyond the place of the Earth, is always swallowed up or hidden in the light of the Sun, yet remains essentially darkness. We see this in the night, which is nothing other than a shadow of the coarse globe of the Earth <note>original: "Aardkloot." An archaic Dutch term for the terrestrial sphere.</note> that blocks the rays of the Sun; because the light cannot reach there, the hidden darkness becomes manifest to us. That eternal darkness (meaning the inward kind) is the wrath of God, and the Hell in which the Devils dwell, along with the damned Souls. And that eternally shining light is the Kingdom of Heaven, a dwelling place of the Holy Angels and Souls. In the space of this world, Heaven and Hell are present everywhere, but according to the inward ground <note>term: "inward ground" (inwendigen grond). In 17th-century mysticism, this refers to the deepest spiritual essence of reality or the soul, which exists beyond physical dimensions.</note>.

In God’s children, that Divine working is inwardly manifest, and in the godless, that working of painful darkness.

The place of the eternal Paradise is hidden in this world in the inward ground, and is manifest in the inward man, provided God’s power works within him. From this world, only the four Elements <note>According to the science of the time, all physical matter was composed of four elements: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note> with

<summary>The author explains that the physical world is a visible reflection of a spiritual interior, where Heaven and Hell coexist as "inward" realities manifest through light and darkness.</summary>
<keywords>microcosm, macrocosm, light and darkness, wrath of God, Paradise, inward ground, spiritual manifestation</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page concludes a sentence from the previous page, explaining that while the physical world is temporary, its spiritual core is eternal. The author then methodically maps the four classical elements—fire, water, air, and earth—to their spiritual counterparts within the divine realm.</meta>

-> THE FIRST PART. <-

<margin>B 3</margin>

...the Stars <note>original: "Gesternt"</note> and the earthly Creatures pass away, like this outward coarse life of all things: That inward power of all Beings remains eternal.

Now, just as we have outward fire here, so there is also an inward Spiritual fire, which is the nature of the eternal Father; for the soul itself is a Magic fire <note>original: "Magies vuur." In this mystical tradition, "magic" refers to the transformative power of the will and spirit rather than sorcery.</note>, and the light that shines from it is its spirit.

And just as we see water here, so there is also a Spiritual water in essence, which is that water of eternal life to which Christ invites us, so that our soul’s fire might be quenched therewith.

And just as there is air here, so there is also an inward air, which is the Holy Spirit.

And just as we have Earth here, so there is also an inward Holy Earth, which is the Divine essence <note>original: "wezentheid"</note> that fills the eternal expanse; which (though it is a Spirit) is thicker and more perceptible <note>original: "begrypelyker." In this context, it means more tangible or substantial to the spiritual senses than the pure, unmanifested Godhead.</note> than the clear Godhead. Out of this Holy Earth grow all kinds of Trees, Herbs, and Flowers—not that one merely compares it to such things, but essentially, just as here in this world, yet not so coarse and tangible, and yet formed and with beautiful colors; it is all Power. In a Heavenly, Spiritual way, it is graspable and flavorful.

If such great beauty and sweetness can be found here, in things where good and evil are mixed together—as an out-birthing <note>original: "uitgeboorte." A term suggesting the physical world was "born out" of a spiritual substance.</note> or revelation of the Kingdom of Light and the Kingdom of Darkness—what then shall it be in the place where the good alone is revealed? The mother and origin of all beauty and sweetness, to which God calls us. He does not want us to remain resting upon the outward; but that we should be guided further through it, to attain the eternal beauty and sweetness.

<meta>This page transitions from the metaphysical discussion of the four spiritual elements on the previous page to a devotional dialogue between Jesus and the Soul. <header>JESUS AND THE SOUL.</header>

### *The Soul finds itself.*

When I lost the world's refuge,
I found myself in a Wilderness;
There the hideous Monster appeared,
Composed of horrors and of sins.
Now we see the high and deep difference,
And how we are the opposite of the Lord;
We find flesh, and self-will,
Which desire against God and His will.
We find Devil, Hell, and Death,
And everything that is horrific to God;
What counsel is there in this highest need?
We find it thus: If God did not invite <note>original: "nooden." In this context, it means to call or beckon the soul toward grace.</note> us,
And draw us, we would never dare,
For shame, to come before His holy face.
O woe! o alas! my God, my Lord!
What has taken the place of the heart!
The Old Man <note>original: "Ouden Mensch." A theological term referring to the fallen, sinful nature of humanity that must be "put off" to achieve spiritual rebirth.</note>, that wicked child,
Driven forward by the governance of the Stars <note>original: "regiment der Sterren." In the mystical tradition of Jacob Boehme and his followers, the "stars" represent the temporal, physical influences of the cosmos that rule over the unregenerate person.</note>,
Is closely befriended with the Devil;
Those are two who lie in wait for my life.
Ah, Treader of the Serpent <note>original: "Slangen-treeder." A title for Jesus Christ, referring to the promise in Genesis 3:15 that the "seed of the woman" would crush the head of the serpent.</note>, stand by me,
I, poor Soul, I fight for your honor:
I for my God, and God for me,
Thus we shall yet defy the enemy.

## Divine Answer.

*Though your sins were as scarlet, they shall*
*become white as snow. Isaiah 1:18.*
*Come hither unto me, all you who are weary and burdened,*
*and I will give you rest. Matthew 11:28.*

### On the IV. Emblem.

*Of finding oneself, and of true sincerity.*

When the spark of God's love becomes manifest in the Soul, she beholds herself, with her will and her works, and becomes aware that she stands in Hell, in the wrath of God; she confesses that she is a misshapen Monster <note>The author uses "Monster" to describe the soul's state when corrupted by sin, contrasting it with the "Image of God."</note> before God and the Kingdom of Heaven. At this, she is so greatly terrified that a great anguish awakes within her; but she shall not despair. Christ calls her to conversion; let her but come, for He wishes to deliver her from the image of the Serpent. Do not search far and wide for the right way.

One does not find the new rebirth <note>term: "wedergeboorte" (rebirth). A central concept in 17th-century Pietism, referring to a radical internal transformation by the Holy Spirit.</note> in dispute, nor in any kind of worldly ingenuity. You must let go of everything in this world, however brightly it may glitter, and enter into yourself. Simply gather your sins, in which you are imprisoned, together into one heap, and flee into the mercy of God and toward God; pray to Him for forgiveness and for the illumination of His Spirit.

Do not dispute for long, but be earnest: for Heaven must roar <note>original: "brieselen." An archaic term suggesting a rushing, storm-like sound or a powerful agitation of the heavens.</note>, and Hell must tremble, and indeed it happens. You must set all your senses, along with your cleverness and everything that comes in your way, upon this: that you will not let go of God. May He then bless you; just as Jacob [wrestled] the whole night

<summary>This page offers a spiritual commentary on the fourth emblem, urging the soul to turn away from worldly cleverness and toward a sincere, inward repentance. It emphasizes that salvation is found through personal anguish over sin and a steadfast refusal to let go of God's mercy.</summary>
<keywords>repentance, new birth, spark of love, spiritual anguish, Jacob's wrestling, sincerity, conversion, internal transformation</keywords>

wrestled with God. When, although your conscience says purely "no, God does not want you," yet I still want Him; I will not let go of You, even if I am carried to the grave: My will be Your will, I want what You, LORD, want; and even if all the Devils stood around you and spoke, "Stop, it is enough for now"; you must say, "No, my mind and will shall not depart from God; they shall remain eternally in God." His love is greater than all my sins. If you, Devil and World, have this mortal body in your prison, then I have my Savior and Rebirther in my Soul; He shall give me a Heavenly body that remains eternally.

Just try it in this way, and you shall experience wonders; you shall soon receive one within you <note>A reference to the Holy Spirit or the "inner Christ" who assists the believer in prayer.</note> who shall help you to wrestle, struggle, and pray: and even if you do not know many words, that is of no consequence, even if you only knew that one word of the Publican <note>original: "Tollenaars." Referring to the humble tax collector in the parable from Luke 18:13.</note>:

->*O God, be merciful to me, a poor sinner:*<-

whenever your will is set in God with all ingenuity and senses; so as not to let go of Him, even if body and soul should shatter; thus you hold God, and break through Death, Hell, and Heaven, and enter into Jesus Christ’s Temple, against the resistance of all Devils: God's wrath cannot keep you, however great and powerful it may be within you; and even if body and soul burned in the wrath and stood in the midst of Hell, among all Devils, yet you would tear yourself out of there and come into Christ's Temple; there you obtain the highly noble and high-worthy Pearl Crown.

<header>JESUS AND THE SOUL.</header>

### The Soul being deeply in love with the noble virtue of meekness.

Meekness <note>original: "Zachtmoedigheid." This term implies a combination of gentleness, patience, and humble submission to God's will.</note>, so widely and highly praised.
O beautiful Flower! Princess of all flowers,
You I love, though you grow beneath the cross,
On a mountain, so wide and far from home,
Still I hope to find you and attain you;
We comfort ourselves on the steep and hard paths;
You are the Flower that JESUS loves to smell,
When I have plucked you with this hand,
And wear you as an ornament upon my breast,
Then first shall I please my Bridegroom <note>In the mystical tradition, the "Bridegroom" is Jesus and the "Bride" is the human soul.</note>:
My God, my Love, my JESUS, all my good;
Then I hear him so kindly in my soul;
For if he is ever to speak his words to me,
Then all storms within me must have retreated;
When stillness dwells in my deepest ground <note>original: "diepsten grond." A term used by Christian mystics to describe the essential core of the soul where the human meets the divine.</note>,
Then God's word flows into me, from his mouth,
And teaches my Spirit how she has to conduct herself,
If she wishes to please her Bridegroom.
Meekness that pierces through my heart,
How my Soul is so very much in love with you.

<header>JESUS AND THE SOUL.</header>

### On the V. Emblem.
### Of Meekness.

The Soul who desires to please Jesus, the most sweet and friendly Bridegroom, and wishes to be beautiful in his eyes, must wear the most noble and exceedingly beautiful little Flower of meekness <note>original: "zachtmoedigheid." In this context, it implies a gentle, patient humility that submits to divine will without resistance.</note> upon her breast.

This little Flower does not grow in her own garden; but far from home, far from carnal ease, self-will, self-righteousness, and vain glory, upon a high mountain—there it grows beneath the cross, in heat and cold, in rain and wind; and, constantly bowing and bending itself, it lets every storm rustle over it.

Whoever now desires to have this little Flower must go there to seek it; it is very beautiful in the eyes of the Bridegroom, and for this reason he has exhorted us toward it through his entire teaching and life.

Meekness is very near to God; for the meek hear the voice of God, which hasty and angry people never hear: for when the wind storms, so that the windows and doors creak, one cannot hear well. If you are now to hear that Fatherly, hidden, secret Word, which is spoken in the holy inner ground <term>inner ground: "inwendigen grond," a mystical term referring to the "scintilla animae" or the deepest, quietest center of the soul where God communicates with man</term> within you, then all haste and turbulence must lie down, and you must be a meek little Sheep, and acknowledge your great failings, and leave them; hearing

<summary>This page explains that the virtue of meekness is not found in personal comfort but grown "beneath the cross" through suffering and submission. It emphasizes that a quiet, meek soul is the only vessel capable of hearing God's "hidden Word" within the inner self.</summary>

...according to this voice with quiet Meekness; this is hidden from all those who are not Sheep.

The Son of God is Himself the true Meekness, who fulfills the eternal Father in the bottomless depth of eternity; if we then enter into Meekness, so we enter into Christ. In the inward Kingdom of God there is nothing but pure Meekness and humility; there is no fury, no wrath, nor pride; therefore, whoever desires to enter into that same Kingdom, let him be truly earnest in dying to all grimness <note>original: "grimmigheid," referring to a state of bitter, hot-tempered anger or malice</note> and learning from JESUS CHRIST Meekness and humility of heart.

> Meekness, O cool Source
> Of Life, welling up from God's powers,
> You must soften my soul-fire <term>soul-fire: "Zielenvuur," a concept in Jakob Böhme's mysticism and Pietist thought, suggesting the natural, raw energy of the soul that can either become a hellish fire of anger or a divine light of love</term>,

> The soul-fire lost its brilliance,

> So generous with all good gifts;
> Let your love eternally refresh us

When Heaven gives its blessing,
Of a beautiful Summer-rain.

But all that is high and exalted,
From there the water drains away,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And flows into the lowest valley.
This is something beautiful to teach me:
So flows the generous Spirit of the Lord,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Into the humble and lowly heart:
O Humility, so highly to be praised,
What beautiful flowers your ground bears!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Oh, if only my mountain became a depth! <note>The "mountain" represents the prideful or "high" self, while the "depth" or valley represents the humble state required to catch the "water" of God's grace.</note>
Oh, could I be small and bowed,
And exalt God in my Soul,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What would flow down from those heights
As lovely water into me,
And make my Spirit bloom so joyfully,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Like a beautiful Rose-garden. <term>Rose-garden: "Roozen-hof," a common mystical metaphor for the soul that has been cultivated by divine grace and has become a dwelling place for God.</term>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;There would JESUS my beloved,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Let himself be found so sweet and friendly.

# JESUS AND THE SOUL.

### Upon the VI. Emblem.

#### Of Humility. <note>original: "Ootmoedigheid"</note>

In the spiritual, invisible essence, if one considers it, there is no "above" or "below"; but if one observes the operation of power or the order of things, then God is above all things, and the **creature** <note>original: "creatuur"; in this context, it refers to any created being, including humans</note> is beneath God. Now the Holy Apostle **James** says: *Every good gift and every perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of lights, with whom there is no change, nor shadow of turning.* <note>A translation of James 1:17.</note>

Whoever wishes to receive from God must necessarily be beneath Him, in true humility. If there is anything that is not entirely beneath, it shall not receive. If you look upon yourself, or a thing, or upon someone else [with pride or self-interest], then you are not at the bottom; therefore, you receive nothing.

But if you are completely at the bottom, then you receive fully. It is God’s nature that He gives, and His very being depends upon this—that He gives to us, provided that we are beneath Him. If we are not beneath Him, we do violence to God and kill Him (so to speak; while we cannot truly harm Him, we do it to ourselves, as much as it lies within our power).

Cast yourself beneath God, in a truly humble **disposition**, <note>original: "gelaatenheid"; a key term in Dutch mysticism meaning a state of yieldedness or surrender to God's will</note> and lift up your heart in God, in your recognition of Him.

That word **Father** means a pure **birthing**, <note>original: "louter baaren"</note> and it is a life of all things.

The Father births His Son in the **Soul**; <note>original: "Ziele"</note> for His essence depends upon this: that He births Himself in the Soul. He births His only begotten Son in the Soul, whether it brings her <note>The soul is traditionally referred to with feminine pronouns.</note> joy or sorrow.

Where the Father births His Son in me, there I am that very same Son.

<summary>This page provides a mystical commentary on humility, explaining that the soul must be "beneath" God to receive His gifts. It concludes with the radical spiritual concept of the Father birthing the Son within the humble soul, leading to a mystical union.</summary>

<keywords>humility, spiritual essence, James 1:17, creature, God's nature, birthing the Son, the Soul, mystical union, disposition, yieldedness</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page completes the theological reflection from the previous page regarding our status as children of God. The previous page ended with the definition of the "Father" as a source of "pure birthing," which leads directly into the opening statement here about our identity as sons and heirs.</meta>

->THE FIRST PART.<-

Since we are Sons, we are true **Heirs**. Thus, **salvation** <note>original: "Zaligheid"; refers to a state of spiritual blessedness or beatitude</note> does not consist so much in tasting, nor in loving, nor in knowing, but in being **born anew**; therefore, the more one **dies to oneself** <note>a central mystical concept where one lets go of the ego and selfish desires to make room for God</note>, the more one is rejuvenated and renewed in the true life, and that is that **eternal life**.

Whoever wishes to dwell in Paradise,
Where so many hundred thousand beauties,
As clear as pure crystal,
Flowed through by the eternal Sunshine,
Play so kindly before the Lord,
They must learn true **humility**. <note>original: "ootmoed"</note>
There is no spirit there, however fair, however great,
However high, and richly **divinized**, <term>original: "vergood"; refers to "theosis" or the process of a human soul becoming unified with God’s nature</term>
Who scorns those who are smaller; they all live
In deep humility: the delight
That one takes in the beauty of another,
Increases the joy in which he lives.
O Human! if such a life pleases you,
Let fall all that stands exalted;
Let fall all of your own "something," <note>original: "eygen iet"; a term for the ego or the sense of self-importance</note>
And become as dust and nothing before God;
Then the Godhead shall fulfill you,
And clothe you as an eternal King,
With a royal crown,
Then you shall shine as beautiful as the Sun.

# JESUS AND THE SOUL

### The Soul gazed upon by God's grace.

O kind face of the Lord!
Have you forgotten all of creation <note>original: "gantse kreatuur"; refers to everything created by God as opposed to the Creator Himself</note>,
As it were, in this hour,
To turn Yourself to me alone?
O kind glance of life!
It feels to me now, O my beloved!
As if there were nothing to be found anywhere,
As if there were nothing but You and I.
I am my beloved’s, and He is mine. <note>A reference to the Song of Solomon 6:3, a central text for Christian mystics describing the union of the soul with God.</note>
O little flowers from Paradise,
O beautiful scent, O beautiful food,
Never must Your power fade from me.
Is this that narrow and steep path? <note>Reference to Matthew 7:14; the soul is surprised that the "difficult" path of faith feels so joyful under the influence of grace.</note>
O World, if you saw through my eyes!
You would come flying from your way;
You would surely leave the mud for the gold.
Now I can do without earthly joy,
Now that You, O source of all good,
Well up so sweetly in my heart <note>original: "gemoed"; the inner seat of emotions, spirit, and thought</note>,
I would die a hundred deaths for You.
What I speak here will be understood by those
Who have once been a guest at this feast. <note>original: "te gast gegaan"; refers to the "spiritual banquet" or the direct experience of God's presence.</note>

<meta>This page continues the meditation on the 7th Emblem. The previous page established the soul's intimate encounter with divine light through the imagery of a direct gaze. This page expands on that experience, describing the sensory "sweetness" of grace and contrasting the soul's inner discovery with the futile material pursuits of the world.</meta>

JESUS AND THE SOUL.

### Upon the 7th Emblem.

->*Of spiritual sweetness, or grace, and of the foolishness of men.*<-

**J**ust as one lures a playing child from a dangerous place toward oneself with a beautiful, fine apple or something else; so the Soul, which has now allowed itself to be moved by the drawing <note>original: "trek"; a mystical term referring to the internal divine attraction or "pulling" of God, based on John 6:44.</note> of the Father to look toward God, is lured and drawn by **Jesus** the Son with extraordinary sweetness; then she finds something of that which she did not know to exist in Heaven nor on earth, and all the things of the world become small and of no value to her. The world sees this and thinks she is foolish; but she has found the precious, priceless pearl <note>A reference to the Parable of the Pearl of Great Price in Matthew 13:45-46.</note>, which is worth selling everything for its sake; then, for the first time, the meaning and the truth of these words dawn upon her: *The Kingdom of Heaven is like a treasure hidden in a field.* <note>Matthew 13:44</note> *Come unto me, all ye that labor and are heavy laden, and I will give you rest.* <note>Matthew 11:28; the Dutch "verkwikken" implies a refreshing or reviving of the spirit.</note> Likewise <note>original: "Item"; Latin for "likewise" or "also."</note>, *I will manifest myself to him.* <note>John 14:21</note> Likewise, *I will give him to eat of the hidden manna,* <note>Revelation 2:17</note> and more such sayings of Holy Scripture, which before were as if dead to her, and now she finds them living within her.

Alas! and alas! for the great foolishness of the world, that people exert so much effort, venturing life and limb over great and wild waters to seek earthly Gold in the mountains of far-off lands, and yet that most noble and most precious Gold, which lies hidden in the inner ground <term>inner ground</term> <note>original: "inwendigen grond"; a term in Christian mysticism (Seelengrund) referring to the deepest part of the human soul where the Spark of the Divine resides.</note> of the soul—of which a single glimpse is better and worth more than all the gold of the world—they have no desire to seek.

<meta>catchword: Becomes</meta>

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a child lured by an apple to explain how Christ uses spiritual "sweetness" to draw the soul away from worldly danger. It contrasts the soul's discovery of the "hidden manna" and "inner gold" with the world's exhausting and perilous pursuit of material wealth.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual sweetness, grace, hidden manna, inner ground, pearl of great price, divine attraction, foolishness of the world, Jan Luyken</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the 7th Emblem, moving from the previous page's description of spiritual "sweetness" to a critique of human skepticism. It contrasts the physical effort people expend in mining for material gold with their refusal to "dig" into their own hearts for spiritual treasures.</meta>

# THE FIRST PART.

If a rough mountain is pointed out to them <note>The Dutch "haar" functions here as a plural pronoun ("them") referring to the "men" mentioned in the previous page's heading.</note>, with promises that it contains Gold, they believe it; and they dig there with great zeal and diligence.

But even if the mountain of their Heart is pointed out to a great many of them—even to those who would much rather die than speak an untruth—with promises that a priceless treasure of Gold and Pearls is to be found there for all those who dig there in earnest; they do not believe it. And even if they see the evidence of this truth in others, in their high contentment and contempt for all earthly things, they still do not believe it, saying: these are but imaginations and dreams.

From where does this come, then, that man gives so much more belief to the one than to the other, when he is shown just as much evidence of the truth in both? Oh, that comes from his great coarseness <note>original: "grovigheid"; this implies a state of being spiritually "thick" or dull, weighed down by material concerns.</note>, because, surrendering himself entirely to the outward life, he has become an animal that strives for nothing but to nourish his body; to the inward life, he is entirely blind.

O Man! If you would do as much for the eternal as you do for the temporal! If you would travel far from home for a noble, high-worthy treasure—far from the house of your outward animal life, far from your own evil, perverse Will, broken off from God—and dig deep night and day in the ground of your heart, you would find something for which you would not trade all the kingdoms of this world.

<vocab>human, Gold, Pearls, inward life, God, hearts, eternal, temporal, diligence, dreams/fantasies</vocab>

# JESUS AND THE SOUL.

### *The Soul, left to its own devices, discovers its nothingness.*

Just as a dark cloud covers the radiant sunlight,
So my Beloved now hides His friendly face;
As long as that light shone within me, my heart beat with joy,
Then I was strong, and sang, and leaped along the path of virtue:
But now that this Divine radiance hides itself,
I find myself to be a Worm, made of dust and ashes.
If I encounter even a small obstacle in my path,
I feel the old man <note>A theological term referring to the sinful human nature or the "fallen" self that resists spiritual growth.</note> writhing like a Serpent.
Then the enemy <note>Referring to the Devil or the personification of temptation and despair.</note> attacks sharply with doubt,
And points me back toward the old way,
How anxious I feel! And yet, O Lord,
Though it tastes so bitter to me, I still give You the glory!
You act so rightly and well, in causing me to go without
That which I value more than all the world’s goods:
This punishment fits my guilt, and the multitude of sins,
Which I have bound together from day to day.
Though I may neither see nor feel You, my God,
Nevertheless keep Your eye upon me the whole day through;
Hold me fast in secret, so that I do not flee from You,
But in resignation <note>original: "gelaatenheid"; a key spiritual term meaning a state of yieldedness or surrender to God's will.</note>, with tears and with sighs,
Lament my guilt, and firmly trust that Your death,
And precious blood, shall release my Soul from its distress.

## Divine Answer.

> For a small moment I have forsaken you: but with great mercies will I gather you.
> Isaiah 54:7.
> Those whom I love, I rebuke and chasten.
> Revelation 3:19.

<summary>The Soul laments the loss of spiritual joy and divine presence, recognizing its own inherent weakness and sinfulness when left alone. God responds with scriptural promises that this "forsaking" is brief and a sign of His love and discipline.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual desertion, the old man, resignation, gelaatenheid, divine absence, repentance, Isaiah 54, Revelation 3</keywords>

# JESUS AND THE SOUL.

## On the Eighth Emblem.

### *Concerning the Withdrawal of Grace.*

In Heaven there is always joy, in Hell there is always sorrow; in the world, both exist for a season to test both the good and the wicked. In the Summer the days are clear, and in the Winter they are dark; so it is also with a devout <note>original: "aandachtige," implying a soul focused in deep, mindful prayer</note> Soul: when God’s grace comes and enlightens her, she knows and understands many hidden things; then she sings and shows joy with the great devotion that she feels. But in times of temptation <note>original: "aanvechtinge," referring to spiritual trials or moments of doubt</note>, when the grace of devotion is withdrawn, she is in winter and in the cold, in darkness of the understanding, and in fear of the mind. At such a time, patience is necessary and more pleasing to God; virtues grow in adversity, and the eternal reward becomes greater through patience.

The Soul becomes humble and pure through scourgings <note>A metaphor for spiritual discipline or hardships</note>, she is shamed by pride, and she fades away through vain honor. As long as the Soul lives in the body, she is exercised in both <note>meaning both joy and sorrow, or light and darkness</note> for greater progress in the love of Jesus. Therefore, it is a great art, and a great strength, to use both the good and the evil well. Therefore, my Soul, praise the Lord always; praise your God day and night, and He shall be your great reward on all sides; before God in Heaven, and on Earth all things shall serve you—the prosperous and the adverse, the good and the evil, the joyful and the sad; of which the Apostle Paul says: *And we know that all things work together for good to those who love God. Romans 8:28.* <note>original: "Rom. VIII: 28"</note> Fear

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the pastoral advice for the soul experiencing spiritual "winter" or dryness. While the previous page introduced the concept of grace being withdrawn to test the soul, this page offers practical instructions on how to persevere through this coldness by looking to the example of Christ's own suffering and abandonment on the cross.</meta>

->THE FIRST PART.<-

*...the Lord, you his saints, for those who fear him have no lack. Psalm 34:10.* <note>original: "Psalm XXXIV: 10." The text uses the Vulgate numbering; in most modern Bibles, this is Psalm 34:9.</note>

When you feel that you are dry, cold, and sorrowful in your prayers and meditations, you shall not despair because of this, nor cease from humbly calling upon JESUS; but praise and thank God in the poverty of your spirit, and gladly read this verse for your comfort: *The poor and needy, O Lord! shall praise your name. Psalm 74:21.* <note>original: "Psalm LXXIV: 21."</note> For many holy and devout men have also sometimes been dry, and for a long time forsaken by God, so that they might learn patience and compassion through the tasting of pain and lack; and so that they would not think too highly of themselves during times of devotion and joy.

Look upon the Lamb of God, and learn resignation <term>gelaatenheid: a central concept in Dutch and German mysticism (Gelassenheit), meaning a state of calm, humble surrender to God's will</term>. Our most beloved Lord Jesus Christ was, for our sins, so forsaken by his Father in the winter of dryness (in a helping manner) and by the Divinity (with which he was nonetheless naturally united) that not a single drop of his Divinity came to the aid of his weak, suffering Humanity for even a moment in all his needs and unspeakable suffering. He was, among all men, the most forsaken and without any help, which is called the "descent into Hell." Whoever could follow Christ in such forsaken resignation would be certain of the Kingdom of Heaven. In this abandoned resignation—of God and all creatures—lies hidden the super-essential <term>overwezentlyke: a metaphysical term referring to a reality that exists beyond or above ordinary being or essence</term> eternal Summer: salvation. When it winters well, it also summers well.

->JESUS AND THE SOUL.<-

## *The Soul, assaulted by evil thoughts, appeals to a pure conscience.*

O Prince of the eternal dark realm,
Though you pelt me with filth and mire,
And the slime of coarse blasphemies,
And a hundred other filthy things,
That does not make me unclean before God,
So long as the living fountain,
Springing forth from a pure conscience,
Continually washes away with its streams
That filth, which is flung from the outside.
As long as my Soul feels sorrow over this
In her very ground <term>gronde: in Dutch mysticism, the "ground of the soul" refers to the deepest, most essential part of a person where they meet God</term> and in painful suffering,
Thus we see her fighting faithfully
For her God and for His honor;
For if she no longer loved her God,
Then she would unite herself with the filth;
And the suffering would instantly vanish.
That is the mark by which I recognize
That I am an enemy of sin;
Therefore, you may do as you please,
Your filth cannot touch the mind. <note>original: "gemoed." This refers to the inner heart or disposition.</note>
Praised be God for all eternity,
Who delivers me from your cunning.

# Divine Answer.

I will deliver you from all your uncleannesses. Ezekiel 36:29. <note>original: "Ezech. XXXVI: 29."</note>
*Count it all joy, my brothers, when you fall into various temptations.* James 1:2. <note>original: "Jak. I: 2."</note>

<meta>catchword: In-</meta>

God responds with a promise of deliverance and an exhortation to find joy even in the midst of such trials.</summary>

# JESUS AND THE SOUL.

### On the IX. Emblem.

#### *Of Impure Thoughts.*

And be neither frightened nor grieved, O God-loving person, though you have intrusive impure thoughts and blasphemies; if you do not give your will to them, and it causes you great sorrow, then be at peace and do not make a matter of conscience <note>meaning: do not become overly scrupulous or guilt-ridden</note> out of it. Think that you are being spat upon along with your savior Jesus Christ, and suffer it willingly for his sake. Know that he will not allow anything to happen to you but that it shall serve to your great salvation, provided that you bear yourself patiently therein. Just as fine gold, the more often it passes through the fire, the purer and nobler it becomes: so also the noble person in their suffering, temptation, and inner struggles <term>aanvechtinge: a term for intense spiritual trials or "assaults" of the soul, common in 17th-century devotional literature</term>: for the more these same struggles are impure, evil, and unspeakable, the more the person is thereby purified and refined; for what is against the free Will of man, therein can no mortal sin occur: but it is for the person a preparation for a higher merit and enjoyment in eternal life. Paul says: *No one is crowned, except he who fights valiantly, and perseveres therein until the end.* <note>original: "Niemant en word gekroond, dan die ridderlyk stryd..." a reference to 2 Timothy 2:5</note> For I tell you, whatever is against your free Will, be it as evil and impure as it may—pride, greed, unchastity, or anything else—that does not defile you, but much

<meta>catchword: more</meta>

<summary>This page provides pastoral advice to the believer on how to handle intrusive, blasphemous, or "impure" thoughts. It argues that such thoughts are spiritual trials that purify the soul like gold in fire, and as long as the individual's will resists them, no sin is committed.</summary>
<keywords>impure thoughts, blasphemy, free will, spiritual struggle, purification, Saint Paul, Jan Luyken, Dutch mysticism</keywords>

-># THE FIRST PART.<-

...more cleansing, purifying, and preparing us for our Lord, and for his special grace. Therefore take heart, be joyful, and be neither sad nor melancholy <note>in this context, a state of spiritual despondency or "heaviness" of the soul</note> if wicked, impure thoughts sometimes occur to you; let them be as wicked as they will, do not be troubled by them; for if they occur to you against your will, then let them also fall away again.

And if this happens to you most of all during your prayer, and in your turning toward God; let that be accepted in the name of God, and suffer this spiritual trial <term>aanvechtinge: a term for intense spiritual trials or "assaults" of the soul, common in 17th-century devotional literature</term> and impure filthiness, humbly and with resignation, through the will of God. Know this: a person may carry themselves so humbly herein for God's sake, that they arrive at such grace through it that would have been entirely foreign to them had they not traveled this path. But a person shall not try to help themselves in this opposition and suffering, neither with words nor with works, but solely and purely with God, and they shall bear this with a gentle heart, and not torment themselves because of it, neither inwardly nor outwardly. When it then seems the right time to the heavenly Father, he can without any doubt deliver you from it, and gladden you a thousandfold with himself in exchange for this painful trial. Therefore, conduct yourself gently, and act with enough simple truth, and whatever falls upon you, remain therein helpless in yourself: for whoever wishes to help themselves too much is helped neither by God nor by the truth. To those who love God, says Paul, all things work together for good <note>original: "gedyen alle dingen ten goede," a reference to Romans 8:28</note>.

-># JESUS AND THE SOUL.<-
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

## The Soul in longing, to be shone through with the divine Light.

The dawn, welcomed with longing,
   By all that lives, flows through the entire sky,
And takes the darkness captive in the light,
   Thus is the earth opened and made fruitful:
My Soul is night; you, JESUS, my beloved,
   Are day, why then can your Sunshine
Not flow through my darkness, and devour it,
   Though we are nevertheless close to one another?
The obstacle is not yours, but mine,
   For you, O light! that overcomes all light,
Can do nothing but gleam and shine,
   Through everything that you find thin and open.
There must be a mountain, of many kinds of failings
   And images <note>In 17th-century mysticism, "images" (beelden) refers to worldly distractions or sensory perceptions that clutter the mind and block spiritual focus</note>, entered through the senses,
Thrusting its high peak up to the clouds,
   Which makes a dismal shadow in my Soul.
Oh, if only that were gone! then would my heart bloom,
   And stand like a Meadow <term>Beemd: a lush, low-lying water-meadow</term>, with flower and fruit.
My strong God, you must clear away <note>original: *roeijen*, meaning to uproot or demolish</note> that wall of partition,
   Even if you must strike its peak with a storm.

### Divine Answer.

Awake, you who sleep, and arise from the dead, and Christ shall shine upon you. Ephesians 5:14.

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Emblem X, following the poem where the Soul laments its own internal "mountain" of distractions that blocks the light of Jesus. # JESUS AND THE SOUL.

<gloss>x</gloss>
### On the 10th Emblem.

->Concerning the Universal Divine Sunshine.<-

John the Baptist speaks: *God does not give the Spirit by measure:* <note>original: "God geeft den Geest niet na maate" — a reference to John 3:34</note> From this, one should understand that the gift of God is the Holy Spirit Himself, and these gifts must be measured not by the One who gives them, but by the one who shall receive them.

Therefore, if that receiving Soul is pure and clear of all **created things** <note>In mystical tradition, "created things" (geschapen dingen) refers to anything that is not God—earthly desires, possessions, or ego—which must be cleared away to make room for the divine</note>, it receives much of the Spirit; but if it is hindered and made narrow by worldly things, it receives little.

God does not give His Spirit by measure, but abundantly and richly and equally to all; that such gifts are now taken unequally and appear unequally, God is not the cause of this: but the people who prepare and hold themselves unequally toward it, loving God and their neighbors unequally. Just as the Sun shines equally on all people, so too does God shine on all souls; but whoever covers themselves is not enlightened.

God offers Himself as perfectly to me as to the highest Angel, and were I as prepared as he, I would receive just as much as he. God offers Himself to all creatures, and each creature receives as much of God as it desires. In short, just as the Sun shines on the good and the evil at once, so too God enlightens all equally with His Spirit; but the wicked are never aware of it, and it is taken from them and given to others; but among the pious, each receives according to how they can prepare themselves for it. That is a wicked person who dares to speak such lies against God, saying: God gives more to one than

<summary>A theological commentary arguing that God's grace and the Holy Spirit are offered equally to everyone, much like sunlight, and that any perceived lack of grace is due to the soul's own attachments rather than God's partiality.</summary>
<keywords>John the Baptist, Holy Spirit, divine light, equality of grace, purity of the soul, sun metaphor, spiritual preparation</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the prose commentary on the 10th Emblem. The previous page established the metaphor of the Sun as God's universal grace, arguing that inequality in spiritual gifts stems from the receiver's lack of preparation rather than God's partiality.</meta>

# THE FIRST PART.

...to others, God is no respecter of persons; <note>original: "God is geen aanziender der persoonen" — a reference to Acts 10:34, meaning God does not show favoritism based on status.</note> He gives His gifts to all people equally, just as to the **Prophets** and **Apostles**, so also to us all; just as to **Christ** His Son, so also to us all, no more and no less; there is not a hair’s breadth of difference. But that I am currently not as highly enlightened as Paul and Elijah, or one of the Apostles—God is not to blame for that, but I am, because I do not entirely <term>die to myself</term>, hate and deny myself, and allow myself to be prepared by God for that purpose. <note>The concept of "dying to self" (afsterven) is central to this period’s mysticism; it refers to the process of emptying the soul of ego and worldly attachments to make room for the Divine.</note> Could I in this hour die to myself, in spirit and in nature, internally and externally, I would receive in one hour as much as the highest Apostle, for God is readier to give than I am to receive. And when He gives, He gives Himself entirely; therefore, the gifts of God must not be measured according to the Giver, but according to the receiver.

Observe: the Holy Spirit is not given to you until you are the <term>inborn Son</term> of God. <note>This refers to the "Birth of Christ in the Soul," a mystical state where the believer is so united with God that they share in the Son’s nature.</note> Everything that Christ the Son has from the Father, we also have that same thing; when we are in the Son, then God gives us the Holy Spirit. Apart from this birth, you can indeed receive the Holy Spirit, but it does not remain with you, and it is to us like a person who turns red from shame and then becomes pale again; that is a fleeting occurrence <note>original: "toeval" — in 17th-century Dutch, this refers to an "accident" or temporary property of a thing, rather than its permanent "essence."</note> and it passes away again. But those beautiful, radiant people, who are the **inborn Son**, with them the Holy Spirit remains <term>essentially</term>. <note>The term "essentially" (wezentlijk) suggests the Spirit becomes part of the person's very being or substance, rather than just a passing feeling.</note>

D
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<vocab>respecter of persons, Prophets, Apostles, Christ, die to self, inborn Son, Holy Spirit, essentially</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that God offers His grace and the Holy Spirit equally to every person, including the same level of enlightenment granted to the Apostles. The lack of spiritual depth in an individual is attributed solely to their own failure to "die to self" and prepare their soul for the permanent, essential indwelling of the Spirit.</summary>
